(NLEB) (Myotis septentrionalis) that was captured on-site during mist netting activities is featured to various U.S. Department of
on the front cover and a big brown bat (Eptesicus fuscus) is pictured on the inside cover. While Energy sites, local libraries, and
some may feel these creatures have faces only a mother could love, these bats offer significant local regulators and stakeholders.

NLEB and big brown bats use echolocation to detect and capture their prey. NLEB are also called Brookhaven National Laboratory
“whisper bats” because their echolocation calls are of such a high frequency they cannot be heard Environmental Protection Division
by the insects upon which they feed and are able to sneak up on them. In addition to capturing Attention: SER Project Coordinator
prey on the wing, NLEB can also pick off insects from surfaces, called gleaning. Building 860
                                                                                                        P.O. Box 5000
NLEB are small bats—their bodies ranging from 3.0-3.7 inches in length with a wingspan of 9-11 Upton, NY 11973-5000
inches and weigh 5-9 grams (about 1/8-1/4 ounce). They have long ears and a wing shape that (631) 344-3248
differs from most other bats that allows them to agilely navigate dense forests better than other
species. From spring through fall, they roost in trees under the bark or in crevices and in winter,
they hibernate in caves. Because we don’t have caves on Long Island, it is believed that they
hibernate in man-made structures like old building foundations, crawl spaces, cellars, and perhaps
in tree crevices. Due to their nocturnal behavior and small size, bats are notoriously difficult to
study so there is much we have yet to learn about them.
What we do know is that NLEB and other regional bat species have been suffering steep declines
in recent years. While habitat loss is a contributing factor, the biggest culprit is the introduction
of a fungal infection called white nose syndrome (WNS) (Pseudogymnoascus destructans) that
thrives in the cold, humid environments found in the caves in which many bats hibernate. The
NLEB has been hit particularly hard by this fungus and populations have declined by an estimated
98%, causing them to be listed as an endangered species by the U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service in
2022. Atlantic coastal islands like Long Island have seen slower declines, likely due to the lack of
caves and the resulting inability for these bats to hibernate in large numbers, which may reduce
transmission rates. Thus, these areas are seen as refugia for this species and conservation efforts
are prioritized.
Forest management at the Lab, including prescribed fire and mechanical treatments, may
positively impact this species by improving habitat and by creating snags in which NLEB can roost.
Continued monitoring of this and other bat species will help to inform future management both at
the Lab and throughout the region, and contribute to the limited information we currently have.

 Executive Summary

 INTRODUCTION

 Brookhaven National Laboratory (BNL) is one of ten National Laboratories
 overseen and primarily funded by the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE’s)
 Office of Science (SC).

 BNL advances scientific discovery and innovation through
 world-leading research facilities, interdisciplinary collaboration,
 efficient initiatives, and safe, community-focused operations.

 Long Island Rail Road (LIRR) Yaphank-BNL Station opened.
 Construction of the LIRR station will provide rail access from
 New York City and New Jersey’s major airports.

 Spent $61 million in New York State, supporting
 local and state businesses.

 Introduction
 NATURAL
 Brookhaven ANDLaboratory
 National CULTURAL RESOURCES
 (BNL) maintains a comprehensive Environmental Radiation Monitoring Program
 to verify that Laboratory operations present no risk to the public, workers, or the environment, and to comply
 with applicable U.S. Department of Energy (DOE) and U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) regulations.
 The program measures radionuclides in facility emissions and the environment, including air, water, soil,

 While environmental monitoring measures the concentration of radioactive materials or radiation present in the
 environment, the dose assessment translates those measurements into potential health impacts. Together,
 environmental monitoring and dose assessment demonstrate that radiological impacts from Laboratory
 operations remained well below applicable regulatory limits during 2025.
 Deer, vegetation, and soil sampling results are consistent
 with previous years’ results. The Natural Resource Management Program
 5.1.1  Radiological Protection Program
 is described in the Natural Resource Management Plan (NRMP) (BNL 2021).
 The Environmental Radiation Protection Program (ERPP) implements DOE and U.S. EPA requirements for
 monitoring radiological emissions in the environment. Monitoring data is used to assess potential radiation dose
 to the public and demonstrate compliance with applicable regulatory standards. The monitoring activities and

 An understanding of an environmental baseline is the foundation of natural resource management planning. BNL
 uses digital global positioning systems (GPS) and geographic information systems (GIS) to clearly relate various
 “layers” of geographic information (e.g., vegetation types, soil condition, habitat, forest health, etc.). This is done
 to gain insight into interrelationships between the biotic systems and physical
 2025 conditions at the Laboratory.

 GROUNDWATER PROTECTION

 Groundwater protection efforts remained effective,
 supported by the operation of 10 groundwater treatment systems
 and routine monitoring from 687 permanent monitoring wells
 and installation of 10 temporary wells.

 Groundwater treatment system operations to date have removed
 7,952 pounds of volatile organic compounds (VOCs) and
 35 mCi of Strontium-90 (Sr-90), returning 34 billion gallons
 of treated groundwater to the aquifer.

 PFAS remediation continued, with the operation of two groundwater
 treatment systems removing 1.9 pounds of PFAS, to date.

 A Note from the Editor
 Throughout the SER, there are many references to Brookhaven National Laboratory (BNL),
the U.S. Department of Energy (DOE), and the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA).
 These acronyms, and others that are explained in each chapter, are used interchangeably
  with their spelled-out forms as an aid to readers. The most up-to-date, accurate version
 of this report is online at https://www.bnl.gov/esh/env/ser/.

1-i 2025 Site Environmental Report

 Chapter 1

Introduction

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-1

 Brookhaven National Laboratory (BNL) is one of ten National Laboratories
 overseen and primarily funded by the U.S. Department of Energy’s (DOE’s)
 Office of Science (SC).

 BNL advances scientific discovery and innovation through
 world-leading research facilities, interdisciplinary collaboration,
 efficient initiatives, and safe, community-focused operations.

 Long Island Rail Road (LIRR) Yaphank-BNL Station opened.
 Construction of the LIRR station will provide rail access from
 New York City and New Jersey’s major airports.

 Spent $61 million in New York State, supporting
 local and state businesses.

 1.1

 Laboratory Vision and Mission
 BNL’s vision is accelerating pathways to scientific discovery and technological innovation that transform the
 world. Over the next decade, the Laboratory will build the Electron-Ion Collider (EIC) – the only new collider
 to be constructed worldwide during the next decade – for the pursuit of knowledge about the basic building
 blocks of matter; lead discovery with light-enabled science at the National Synchrotron Light Source II (NSLS-II),
 a build out of its beamline suite, and a possible upgrade to the accelerator to drive advances in a broad range
 of science disciplines; enable an interdisciplinary approach to emergent information science and capabilities
 by taking advantage of the Laboratory’s core expertise; and foster programs that address energy security and
 essential resource independence.

 BNL carries out its mission in an environmentally responsible and safe manner, with the partnership of local, state,
 national, and international communities. The Laboratory conceptualizes, designs, builds, and operates major
 scientific facilities in support of its DOE mission. These facilities serve DOE’s basic research needs and reflect BNL/
 DOE stewardship of national research infrastructure critical for university, industry, and government researchers.

 The Laboratory’s high-level, enduring science and technology (S&T) priorities define and distinguish BNL. They
 fall broadly into the following areas:
 § Nuclear and particle physics to gain a deeper understanding of matter, energy, space, and time;
 § Recognized strengths in advanced materials, catalysis, bioenergy, environmental systems, and
 climate;
 § Advanced computer science, applied math, data science, and computational science to transform
 scientific discovery at BNL’s facilities and enhance its science programs;
 § Advanced and emerging technology with demonstrated strengths in instrumentation, magnet,
 accelerator, and laser S&T;

1-2 2025 Site Environmental Report

 § Transformational user facilities that position the Laboratory and the Nation for continued leadership
 roles in S&T. These facilities are enabled by advanced accelerator S&T; and
 § Application of the results of BNL’s discovery science to address emerging opportunities, including
 clean energy solutions, isotopes, national security solutions, and national emergencies.

Achieving the Laboratory’s vision and mission requires simultaneous excellence in all aspects of BNL’s work from science
and operations to external partnerships with the local, state, and national communities, and beyond. Realizing these
goals is enabled by safe, efficient, and secure operations; workforce development and community outreach that
drives regional partnerships to address national needs.

Each employee, contractor, and guest is expected to take personal responsibility for adhering to BNL’s values
toward environmental protection as stated in the Laboratory’s Code of Conduct.

1.2
Major Initiatives
BNL has identified scientific initiatives that will help realize its larger vision and mission. These initiatives align with
the DOE Strategic Goals in Science, Energy, and Nuclear Security and build on the Laboratory’s core strengths
and capabilities. The breadth of BNL’s core programs serves as the foundation for the seven initiatives. The
initiatives are highly interconnected, utilizing the Laboratory’s user facilities and the S&T capabilities across the
BNL complex.

The seven initiatives are:
 § Nuclear Physics: Uncover the structure of visible matter by constructing and operating the EIC
 at BNL to maintain international leadership in nuclear physics for decades;
 § Clean Energy and Climate: Support fundamental research in basic energy and climate sciences
 to revolutionize grid-scale storage, renewable integration, and the study of atmospheric processes
 to improve climate predictability;
 § Quantum Information Science and Technology: Discover new quantum materials to enhance
 quantum computers and develop an entanglement sharing quantum network as a prototype for
 the first quantum internet;
 § Artificial Intelligence and Data Science: Revolutionize the operation of experiments across
 the sciences at user facilities and in core programs;
 § High Energy Physics: Understand the origin of space, time, and matter with the ATLAS high
 luminosity upgrade at CERN and the future Long Baseline Neutrino Facility/Deep Underground
 Neutrino Experiment;
 § Isotope Production: Accelerate and expand isotope production to ensure the security
 of the Nation’s supply; and
 § Accelerator Science and Technology: Harness the accelerator science expertise at BNL
 to develop new facilities, improve and expand its user facilities, and promote the use of
 accelerators in industry.

In support of these initiatives, the Laboratory operates cutting-edge large-scale facilities for studies in physics,
chemistry, biology, medicine, applied science, and a wide range of advanced technologies. BNL’s world-class
research facilities are also available to university, industry, and government personnel from around the world.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-3

 BNL Main Gate Sign.

 The Laboratory integrates efficient operations and environmental stewardship into all facets of its research and
 operations and is committed to managing its programs in a manner that protects the local ecosystem
 and public health.

 In addition to major research activities, the Laboratory provides expertise and other programs in a range of areas
 including accelerator S&T, biological imaging, homeland and national security, and advanced computation.

 To date, researchers working at BNL have received seven Nobel Prizes, multiple National Medals of Science,
 National Medal of Technology and Innovation, National Academy of Sciences, Enrico Fermi Awards, Wolf
 Foundation Prizes, nearly 40 Research and Development (R&D) 100 Awards, as well as other recognitions for
 discoveries made wholly or partly at BNL. Some significant discoveries and developments made at the Laboratory
 include new forms of matter, subatomic particles, technologies that fuel leading experimental programs around
 the world, and lifesaving medical imaging techniques for diagnosis and treatment of disease.

 1.3
 History and Overview of Major Scientific Facilities
 BNL was founded in 1947 by the Atomic Energy Commission (AEC), a predecessor to the present DOE. The AEC
 provided the initial funding for BNL’s research into peaceful uses of the atom. The objective was to promote basic
 research in the physical, chemical, biological, and engineering aspects of the atomic sciences through the design,
 construction, and operation of large-scale scientific facilities beyond the capabilities of individual institutions.

 Although BNL no longer operates any research reactors, the Laboratory’s first major scientific facility was the
 Brookhaven Graphite Research Reactor (BGRR), which was the first reactor to be constructed in the United
 States following World War II. In operation from 1950 to 1968, the reactor’s primary mission was to produce
 neutrons for scientific experimentation and to refine reactor technology. The High Flux Beam Reactor (HFBR) was
 in operation from 1965 through 1996, and further expanded neutron research capabilities in materials science,
 chemistry, biology, and physics. Both reactors have since been permanently shut down and decommissioned,
 with remaining structures maintained under long-term inspection and surveillance.

 Medical research at BNL began in 1950 with the opening of one of the first hospitals devoted to nuclear
 medicine. It was followed by the Medical Research Center in 1958 and the Brookhaven Medical Research
 Reactor (BMRR) in 1959, the nation’s first reactor designed specifically for medical research, operated from 1959
 until 2000. All spent fuel from the BMRR has been removed and transported off-site. The facility is currently in
 a “cold” shutdown mode as a radiological facility and has entered a period of surveillance and maintenance. In
 August 2022, the BMRR stack was safely demolished.

CERCLA = Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation and Liability Act USEPA = United States Environmental Protection Agency
NASA = National Aeronautics and Space Administration NYSDEC = New York Department of Environmental Conservation
DOE = Department of Energy

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-5

 The Brookhaven Linac Isotope Producer (BLIP) has been in operation since 1972. Positioned at the forefront of
 research into radioisotopes used in cancer treatment and diagnosis, the BLIP produces commercially unavailable
 radioisotopes for use by the medical community and related industries. The BLIP consists of an accelerator
 beam line and target area for generating radioisotopes already in high demand and for developing those required
 at the frontiers of nuclear medicine. In conjunction with this mission, scientists also perform irradiations for non-
 isotope applications and explore opportunities for emerging radioisotope applications.

 High-energy particle physics research at BNL began in 1952 with the Cosmotron, the first particle accelerator
 to achieve billion-electron-volt energies and led to the Laboratory’s first Nobel Prize in 1957. Knowledge gained
 from the Cosmotron led to design improvements and paved the way for construction of the Alternating Gradient
 Synchrotron (AGS), a much larger particle accelerator that became operational in 1960 and allowed scientists
 to accelerate protons to energies that have yielded many discoveries of new particles and phenomena, and
 resulted in three Nobel Prizes. For the complete list of Nobel Prizes, see BNL Nobel Prize Timeline on page 1-7,
 or visit https://www.bnl.gov/bnlweb/history/nobel/.

 The Tandem Van de Graaff accelerator began operating in 1970 and is the starting point of the chain of
 accelerators that provide ions of gold, other heavy metals, and protons for experiments at the Relativistic
 Heavy Ion Collider (RHIC). In 2010, BNL began operating a new heavy ion beam source for use by RHIC and
 the National Aeronautics and Space Administration (NASA) Space Radiation Laboratory (NSRL), the Electron
 Beam Ion Source (EBIS). This large electrostatic accelerator can provide researchers with beams of more than
 40 different types of ions ranging from hydrogen to uranium. By simulating the effects of radiation both in space
 and on the ground, scientists and engineers from several other laboratories and companies are improving the
 reliability of computers.

 RHIC began operation in 2000. Inside this two-ringed particle accelerator, two beams of gold ions, heavy
 metals, or protons circulate at nearly the speed of light and collide, head-on, releasing large amounts of energy.
 By smashing particles together to recreate the conditions of the early universe, scientists can explore the most
 fundamental building blocks of matter as they existed just after the Big Bang. This research unlocks secrets of
 the force that holds together 99% of the visible universe everything from stars to planets and people and triggers
 advances in science and technology that have applications in fields from medicine to national security.

 RHIC has been continuously upgraded and its productivity now exceeds its initial design by 100 times. The most
 recent upgrade is the Low-Energy RHIC Electron Cooling project, which supports a new research program.

 In 2022, RHIC completed construction and installation of a brand-new house-sized particle detector. This new
 detector, known as sPHENIX – together with RHIC’s newly enhanced STAR detector – greatly advances physicists’
 ability to study subtle details of quark-gluon plasma, a remarkable form of matter that filled the early universe. RHIC
 recreates tiny specks of this early universe particle “soup” thousands of times each second by colliding the nuclei of
 atoms at nearly the speed of light.

 In 2020, the DOE selected BNL as the site for the EIC, a next-generation accelerator. A collaboration between BNL
 and Thomas Jefferson National Accelerator Facility, the EIC will be built by reusing one of the RHIC accelerators
 and the addition of an electron accelerator to allow collisions of electrons and ions, giving scientists a rare and
 exciting opportunity to explore and study the internal structures of atomic nuclei. What we learn from the EIC could
 power the technologies of tomorrow.

 The NSRL became operational in 2003. It is jointly managed by DOE-SC and NASA’s Johnson Space Center.
 The NSRL uses heavy ions to simulate space radiation to study the effects on biological specimens, such
 as cells, tissues, and DNA, as well as industrial materials and electronics. Studies are conducted to identify
 materials and methods that will reduce the risks astronauts face on future long-term space missions.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-7

 1.3.1 User Facilities
 BNL also hosts several major user facilities
 that support multidisciplinary research. The
 National Synchrotron Light Source II (NSLS-
 II), which replaced the original Nobel Prize
 winning NSLS in 2014, provides ultra-bright
 x-ray, ultraviolet, and infrared light offering
 an array of sophisticated imaging techniques
 to capture atomic-level “pictures” of a wide
 variety of materials, from biological molecules
 to semi-conductor devices. NSLS-II has a
 nanometer-scale resolution — a key resource
 for researchers at BNL’s Center for Functional
 Nanomaterials (CFN) — that will enhance
 the development of next-generation energy
 technologies and improve imaging of complex
 protein structures. The NSLS-II has design
 National Synchrotron Light Source II. space for up to 60 beamlines of which 29 have
 currently been built and are operational.

 BNL’s CFN is one of five Nanoscale Science Research Centers funded by DOE-SC and provides state-of-the-art
 tools for creating and exploring the properties of materials with dimensions spanning just billionths of a meter.

 CFN scientists are dedicated to atomic-level tailoring that addresses a wide range of energy challenges. CFN
 focus areas include improving solar cells and other electronic nanomaterials; designing more efficient catalysts;
 developing new capabilities and uses for electron microscopy; and nanofabrication based on soft and biological
 nanomaterials—all aided by theory and advanced computation. The CFN building was awarded Leadership in
 Energy and Environmental Design (LEED) Silver certification.

 The 32-megawatt Long Island Solar Farm (LISF) was completed in 2011 in collaboration with BP Solar, Long Island
 Power Authority (LIPA), the State of New York, and other organizations. The LISF generates renewable energy that
 helps meet peak electricity demands during
 the summer months from an increase in air
 conditioning use, powers approximately 4,500
 homes, and supports New York State’s clean
 energy efficiency goals.

 In addition to scientific infrastructure, BNL
 has made significant investments in efficient
 and modern facilities including the NSLS-II,
 CFN, and the Interdisciplinary Science Building
 (ISB). These energy efficient facilities provide
 labs, offices, and support functions to bring
 together a broad spectrum of researchers,
 including industry, universities, and other
 National Laboratories. The ISB’s scientific
 mission fosters energy research, focusing
 on the effective uses of renewable energy The Long Island Solar Farm (LISF), located on the Brookhaven
 through improved conversion, transmission, National Laboratory site, began delivering power to the grid in
 and storage. The ISB was awarded LEED November 2011.

Gold certification. BNL is establishing itself as a global leader in tackling the challenges of Big Data, building on
existing expertise, capabilities, and investments in computational science and data management, and enabling
scientific discovery in large-scale experimental environments.

Based on the original NSLS facility, and established in 2016, the Computational Science Initiative (CSI) oversees
the following areas: Computer Science and Applied Mathematics, Computing for National Security, Scientific
Data and Computing Center, Computation and Data-Driven Discovery, and the Computational Science Lab.
The CSI takes a multidisciplinary, collaborative approach to its research, targeting challenges in cooperation with
fellow researchers in science, national security, and industry, both at home and abroad. The CSI has focused on
timely analysis and interpretation of high-volume, high-velocity heterogeneous data, providing solutions for the
national and international scientific community. These efforts are now being augmented by the CSI’s growing
high-performance computing capabilities.

Looking ahead, BNL continues to modernize its site through strategic developments like Discovery Park, a
transformative vision for the Laboratory’s entryway to welcome visitors, provide housing for facility users and
guests, and offer new opportunities for private industry to collaborate. It also provides an opportunity for
economic and community development on Long Island, New York State, and beyond. The Discovery Park
concept is built upon responsible development that aligns with the interests of the DOE, BNL, and the regional
New York and Long Island economies.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-9

 1.4
 Facilities and Operations
 Most of the Laboratory’s principal facilities are located near the center of the site. The developed area is
 approximately 1,820 acres consisting of the following:
 § 500 acres originally developed by the Army as part of Camp Upton, and still used for offices
 and other operational buildings;
 § 200 acres occupied by large, specialized research facilities;
 § 520 acres used for outlying facilities, such as the Sewage Treatment Plant (STP), ecology field,
 housing facilities, and fire breaks;
 § 400 acres of roads, parking lots, and connecting areas; and
 § 200 acres occupied by the LISF.

 The location of the major scientific facilities at BNL are shown on Figure 1-1. Additional facilities, shown on
 Figure 1-2 and briefly described below, support BNL’s science and technology mission by providing basic utility
 and environmental services.
 § Central Chilled Water Facility (CCWF). This plant provides chilled water sitewide for air
 conditioning and process refrigeration via underground piping. The plant has a large refrigeration
 capacity and reduces the need for local refrigeration plants and air conditioning.
 § Central Steam Facility (CSF). This facility provides high-pressure steam for heating and
 site-wide processes. Either natural gas or fuel oil can be used to produce the steam, which is
 conveyed to other facilities through underground piping. Condensate is collected and returned to
 the CSF for boiler make-up to conserve water and energy.
 § Fire Station. The Fire Station houses six response vehicles. The BNL Fire Rescue Group provides
 on-site fire suppression, emergency medical services, hazardous material response, salvage, and
 property protection.
 § Major Petroleum Facility (MPF). This facility provides reserve fuel for the CSF during times of
 peak operation. With a total capacity of 1.9 million gallons of capacity, the MPF stores different
 types of residual fuel (e.g., No. 2, 4, and 6). The 1997 conversion of CSF boilers to burn both
 natural gas and oil has significantly reduced the Laboratory’s reliance on oil as a sole fuel source
 when other fuels are more economical, and the primary use of natural gas has significantly
 reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.
 § Sewage Treatment Plant (STP). This plant treats sanitary and certain process wastewater from
 BNL facilities prior to discharge into groundwater recharge beds, similar to the operations of a
 municipal STP. The plant has a design capacity of 2.3 million gallons per day. Effluent is monitored
 and controlled under a permit issued by the New York State Department of Environmental
 Conservation.
 § Waste Management Facility (WMF). This facility manages the wastes generated from BNL’s
 research and operations activities. The facility was built with advanced environmental protection
 systems and features and began operation in December 1997.
 § Water Treatment Plant (WTP). The potable water treatment plant has a capacity of five
 million gallons per day. Potable water is obtained from four on-site wells. Water pumped from
 a supply well located in the western section of the site is treated at the WTP to remove naturally
 occurring iron and then injected with lime to adjust the pH, and with sodium hypochlorite for

1-12 2025 Site Environmental Report

1.5
Location, Local Population, and Local Economy
BNL, the only multi-disciplinary scientific National Laboratory located in the Northeast and one of New York
State’s largest centers of scientific research, places special emphasis on growing the technology-based
elements of the Long Island economy. The future competitiveness of New York’s economy depends on its
capacity for innovation, and Brookhaven represents a uniquely valuable resource both as a major science-based
enterprise, and as a source of discoveries that drive entrepreneurs and innovators.

The Laboratory’s 5,265-acre site is located in Brookhaven Town, Suffolk County, New York, approximately 65
miles east of New York City. In 2025, BNL employed 2,796 full-time employees and 76 part-time staff, comprised
of scientists, engineers, technicians, and support staff. In addition, the Laboratory annually hosts an estimated
7,300 visiting scientists, facility users, and students from universities, industries, and government agencies,
who often stay in apartments and dormitories on-site or in nearby communities. BNL strengthens Long Island’s
position as a center of innovation in energy, materials sciences, nanotechnology, and other fields crucial to the
growth of New York State’s economy. With its geographic location and fiscal year 2025 budget of $930 million,
the Laboratory has a significant economic impact on New York State, In 2025, Laboratory employee salaries,
wages, and fringe benefits accounted for approximately $540 million, or 60% of its total budget. Supporting local
and state businesses whenever possible, the Laboratory spent $61 million in New York State with $45 million of
that amount spent in Nassau and Suffolk counties.

1.6
Environmental Setting
BNL is situated within the Central Pine Barrens region of Long Island, a New York State designated area of significant
ecological importance that plays a critical role in the protection of Long Island’s groundwater resources. The site
lies along the western edge of the Peconic watershed, with marshy areas in the northern and eastern section of the
rivers’ headwaters.

Geologically, the area consists of thick glacial sand and gravel that allows water to penetrate the deposits, resulting in
minimal surface runoff unless precipitation is intense. The sandy deposits store large quantities of water in the Upper
Glacial aquifer. On average, about half of the annual precipitation is lost through evapotranspiration, while the rest
percolates through the soil to recharge the groundwater (Franke & McClymonds, 1972; Aronson & Seaburn, 1974).

The local hydrology is closely tied to the Upper Glacial aquifer. Depending on precipitation conditions, the Peconic
River both recharges and receives water from the aquifer. The terrain on-site is gently rolling, with elevations varying
between 44 and 120 feet above mean sea level.

The Long Island Regional Planning Board and Suffolk County have identified the Laboratory site as overlying a
deep-flow recharge zone for Long Island groundwater (Koppelman, 1978). Precipitation and surface water that
recharge within this zone have the potential to replenish the Magothy and Lloyd aquifer systems lying below the
Upper Glacial aquifer. It has been estimated that up to two-fifths of the recharge from rainfall moves into the deeper
aquifers.

This groundwater system is the primary source of drinking water for both on- and off-site private and public supply
wells and has been designated a sole source aquifer system by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency, Thus, the
protection of groundwater resources is an important consideration in site planning and operations. For more details
on the site’s hydrogeology and groundwater system, refer to Chapter 7.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 1-13

       1.6.1 Climate
 Meteorological Services (MET Services) at BNL has been recording on-site weather data since August 1948.
 MET Services is responsible for the maintenance, calibration, data collection, and data archiving for the weather
 instrumentation network at BNL. Measurements include wind speed, wind direction, temperature, rain fall,
 barometric pressure, relative humidity, and solar irradiance.

 The Laboratory is broadly influenced by continental and maritime weather systems. Locally, the Long Island
 Sound, Atlantic Ocean, and associated bays influence wind directions and humidity and provide a moderating
 influence on extreme summer and winter temperatures.

 The prevailing ground-level winds at BNL are from the southwest during the summer, from the northwest during
 the winter, and about equally from those two directions during the spring and fall (Nagle 1975, 1978). Figure 1-3
 shows the 2025 annual wind rose for BNL, which depicts the annual frequency distribution of wind speed and
 direction, measured at an on-site meteorological tower at heights of 33 feet (10 meters) and 300 feet (85 meters)
 above land surface.
 Figure 1-3. BNL Wind Rose (2025)

 Wind Rose for Jan 1 to Dec 31, 2025 Wind Rose for Jan 1 to Dec 31, 2025
 taken at the 85m height. taken at the 10m height.

 In 2025, the average yearly temperature for this area of Long Island was 52.3 °F. The coolest month of the year,
 January, had a monthly average temperature of 29.8 °F while the warmest month of the year, July, had a monthly
 average temperature of 72.3 °F. Figures 1-4 and 1-5 show the 2025 monthly mean temperatures and the
 historical annual mean temperatures, respectively.

 2.1
 Integrated Safety Management and EMS
 BNL’s ISMS integrates environmental protection, pollution prevention, safety, health, and quality management
 into all work planning and execution. The purpose of the ISMS is to integrate the Department of Energy’s (DOE’s)
 five Core Functions and seven Guiding Principles into all work processes. The five Core Functions, as defined by
 48 CFR Subpart 970.5223-1, Integration of Environment, Safety, and Health into Work Planning and Execution
 for contractors and DOE O 450.2, Chg 1 (MinChg), Integrated Safety Management, (2011) for DOE elements,
 are:
 § Define the scope of work: Missions are translated into work, expectations are set, tasks are
 identified and prioritized, and resources are allocated.
 § Analyze the hazards: Hazards associated with the work are identified, analyzed, and categorized.
 § Develop and implement hazard controls: Applicable standards and requirements are
 identified and agreed upon; controls to prevent and mitigate hazards are identified; the safety
 envelope is established; and controls are implemented.
 § Perform work within controls: Readiness is confirmed and work is performed safely.
 § Provide feedback and continuous improvement: Feedback information on the adequacy of
 controls is gathered; opportunities for improving the definition and planning of work are identified
 and implemented.

2-2 2025 Site Environmental Report

The seven Guiding Principles, also defined by DOE P 450.4A, Chg 1, are:
 § Line management responsibility for safety: Line management is directly responsible for the
 protection of the workers, the public, and the environment.
 § Clear roles and responsibilities: Clear and unambiguous lines of authority and responsibility for
 ensuring safety are established and maintained at all organizational levels within the Department
 and its contractors.
 § Competence commensurate with responsibilities: Personnel possess the experience,
 knowledge, skills, and abilities that are necessary to discharge their responsibilities.
 § Balanced priorities: Resources are effectively allocated to address safety, programmatic, and
 operational considerations. Protecting the workers, the public, and the environment is a priority
 whenever activities are planned and performed.
 § Identification of safety standards and requirements: Before work is performed, the
 associated hazards are evaluated and an agreed upon set of safety standards and requirements
 is established which, if properly implemented, will provide adequate assurance that the workers,
 public, and environment are protected from adverse consequences.
 § Hazard controls tailored to work being performed: Administrative and engineering controls
 to prevent and mitigate hazards shall be tailored to the work being performed and associated
 hazards.
 § Operations authorization: The conditions and requirements to be satisfied for operations to be
 initiated and conducted shall be clearly established and agreed upon.

2.1.1 BNL Values
The integrated processes within ISMS contribute to BNL maintaining an effective EMS based on the “Plan-
Do-Check-Act” improvement cycle. Built on the ISO 14001 standard framework, the system requires BNL to
develop and implement environmental commitments, monitor progress, take corrective actions, and review the
system periodically to ensure its continued suitability, adequacy, and effectiveness.

The cornerstone of an EMS is a commitment to environmental protection at the highest levels of an organization.
BNL’s environmental commitments are incorporated into the Laboratory’s Vision and Values Statement. The
statement communicates the Laboratory’s intentions and principles regarding overall environmental performance,
and provides a framework for planning and action, and is included in all employee, guest, and contractor training
programs. The values are on the BNL website at https://intranet.bnl.gov/conduct/.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 2-3

 2.2
 EMS Planning and Performance
 2.2.1 Environmental Aspects
 BNL identifies the aspects that can impact the environment and determines which of those impacts are
 significant. The Laboratory’s criteria for significance are based on actual and perceived impacts of its operations
 and on regulatory requirements and are incorporated into the work planning process so they are identified and
 reviewed for all work. Environmental professionals work closely with Laboratory personnel to ensure that work
 planning activities thoroughly capture all aspects, requirements, and associated environmental controls. Annual
 work planning reviews ensure that aspects and impacts are evaluated on a regular basis and continue to reflect
 stakeholder concerns and changes in regulatory requirements.

 2.2.2 Objectives
 The Laboratory’s 2025 EMS objectives focused on maintaining a strong and compliant EMS, supporting major
 Laboratory infrastructure projects, advancing long-term groundwater stewardship activities, and strengthening
 operational sustainability initiatives. Objectives included auditing, conducting programmatic self-assessments
 and updating key environmental subject areas and procedures. The plan also emphasized long-term stewardship
 responsibilities under the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA),
 including groundwater treatment system management, per- and poly fluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) and 1,4-
 dioxane investigations, permit renewals, treatment system engineering design work, and regulator engagement
 regarding closure and shutdown petitions. Additional priorities included supporting the building demolition
 projects and related remedial activities and obtaining environmental permits and regulatory approvals for the
 Electron-Ion Collider (EIC) project. Other efforts included strengthening internal Environment, Safety, and Health
 (ES&H) support processes, and onboarding new staff.

 The Laboratory will continue to focus on advancing operational excellence, major infrastructure initiatives,
 environmental stewardship, and modernization of environmental compliance and information management
 programs. Key efforts include support of the EIC project and the Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider (RHIC) Removal
 and Repurposing Project while coordinating with regulators on wetlands, endangered species, and National
 Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) requirements. Long-term stewardship activities will include completion of the
 CERCLA Five-Year Review process, groundwater treatment system evaluations, PFAS characterization activities,
 and development of engineering designs and cost estimates for potential remediation system enhancements.
 The Environmental Protection Division (EPD) will also continue environmental liability reduction efforts associated
 with building construction and demolition while maintaining regulator, community, and stakeholder engagement
 during the remediation and demolition progress.

 Operational modernization and efficiency initiatives will also be advanced during 2026 through development
 of new programmatic agreements with regulators, notably the New York State Historic Preservation Office
 (NYSHPO), as well as modernization of the division’s website and SharePoint collaboration tools, and increased
 integration of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, such as ChatGPT, into environmental reporting, compliance
 tracking, and data analysis activities.

2-4 2025 Site Environmental Report

2.2.3 Monitoring and Measurement
BNL’s EMS specifies requirements for conducting general surveillance to determine impact from site
operations to the environment. DOE O 458.1 Admin Chg. 5, (2025), Radiation Protection of the Public and
Environment, requires DOE sites to maintain surveillance monitoring for determining radiological impacts, if
any, to the public and environment from site operations.

BNL’s Environmental Monitoring Program is a comprehensive, site-wide program that identifies potential pathways
for exposure of the public and employees, evaluates the impact activities have on the environment, and ensures
compliance with environmental permit requirements. The Environmental Monitoring Plan (EMP) (BNL 2025) defines
how the Laboratory will monitor effluents and emissions to ensure the effectiveness of controls, adherence to
regulatory requirements, and timely identification and implementation of corrective measures. The EMP uses
the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) Data Quality Objective approach for documenting the decisions
associated with the monitoring program. In addition to the required triennial update, an annual electronic update
is also prepared. The monitoring programs are reviewed and revised, as necessary, to reflect changes in permit
requirements, changes in facility-specific monitoring activities, or the need to increase or decrease monitoring
based on a review of previous analytical results. As shown in Table 2-1, there were 6,040 sampling events of
groundwater, potable water, precipitation, air, plants and animals, soil, sediment, and discharges under the EMP.
Specific sampling programs for the various media are described further in Chapters 4 and 5. The EMP addresses
three components: compliance, restoration, and surveillance monitoring.

2-6 2025 Site Environmental Report

2.2.4 Assurance
To periodically verify that the EMS is operating as intended, assessments are conducted as part of the
Laboratory’s Contractor Assurance System (CAS). Self-assessment is the systematic evaluation of internal
processes and performance and includes both systematic assessments and regulatory compliance audits.
The CAS is augmented by programmatic external audits conducted by the DOE. Brookhaven Science
Associates (BSA) staff and subcontractors also perform periodic independent reviews, and a team consisting
of certified assessors from other Battelle Laboratories independently assess and verify BNL’s EMS program
through a Community of Practice (CoP), further discussed in Section 2.3.1. BNL is subject to extensive
oversight by external regulatory agencies (see Chapter 3 for details). In 2025, the assessors came on-site
and there were no findings. The Laboratory earned a “Green” rating on the DOE Annual EMS Report,
indicating strong performance against its environmental management objectives.

2.3
Environmental Operating Experience
2.3.1 Collaboration and Continuous Improvement
In 2025, BNL hosted a peer review through the Battelle CoP, bringing together environmental professionals from
six National Laboratories to exchange best practices, discuss regulatory challenges, and identify opportunities to
improve environmental management programs.

During the visit, staff presented on chemical management and legacy waste prevention, natural and cultural
resource programs, contractor assurance, navigating a changing regulatory environment, operational
improvements, EMS, and strategies for long-term environmental stewardship. The review included tours at the
National Synchrotron Light Source II, the Center for Functional Nanomaterials, and the National Aeronautics
and Space Administration (NASA) Space Radiation Laboratory in addition to field trips that highlighted
the Laboratory’s prescribed fire management program, the wildlife of the Long Island Pine Barrens, and
Brookhaven’s historic buildings, guided by Natural and Cultural Resource managers.

The Laboratory’s Office of Institutional Improvement applies human performance improvement principles to
streamline work processes, reduce waste, and improve organizational effectiveness. Within EPD, these methods
have been used to improve management of empty compressed gas cylinders and streamline processes for
managing excess soils generated during site development activities.

BNL’s sustainable procurement program was recognized with the Green Electronics Council’s Electronic Product
Environmental Assessment Tool (EPEAT) Purchaser Award for its commitment to purchasing environmentally
preferrable electronic products.

During 2025, Brookhaven also opened the new Science User Support Center (SUSC), the first completed
facility in Discovery Park. The building was designed in accordance with DOE Guiding Principles for Sustainable
Buildings and incorporates numerous sustainable design features, including stormwater management
through on-site collection and bioretention, native and drought-tolerant landscaping that minimizes irrigation
requirements, extensive use of recycled construction materials such as structural steel, and a building orientation
and high-performance glazing that maximize natural daylight while reducing energy demand. Energy-efficient
LED lighting with occupancy and daylight controls further reduces electricity consumption while providing a
comfortable environment for users.

 2025 Site Environmental Report 2-7

 The Laboratory’s Natural and Cultural Resources Program continues to preserve Brookhaven’s historical
 resources through partnerships with local museums and historical organizations. During 2025, these
 collaborations highlighted Brookhaven’s scientific achievements and the history of Camp Upton through exhibits
 featuring Laboratory artifacts and historical collections. Scientists also worked closely with museum curators to
 ensure the scientific and historical accuracy of the exhibits.

 The Laboratory’s Wildfire Management Program is one such example, having become a model for effective
 wildfire risk reduction in the 13 years since the 2012 Crescent Bow Fire. Through the Natural and Cultural
 Resources Program, BNL has implemented a proactive forest management strategy that includes mechanical
 fuel reduction treatments to prepare designated management units for prescribed burns. These activities reduce
 hazardous fuel loads, decreasing wildfire risk to both the Laboratory and the neighboring Ridge community. BNL
 works in close partnership with the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (NYSDEC), the
 Long Island Central Pine Barrens Commission, and other agencies to safely plan and conduct prescribed burns.
 The effectiveness of this program has been recognized by the New York Wildfire and Incident Management
 Academy, which has selected BNL as the site of its annual training academy.

 These collaborative efforts demonstrate BNL’s commitment to continuous improvement of the EMS by
 collaborating and advancing environmental stewardship and operational performance.

 2.4
 Efficient Operations
 The Laboratory incorporates efficiency into its operations to prevent incidents and conserve resources through
 the reuse of materials whenever possible. Waste management is one example, with programs that manage
 a variety of waste types through segregation and recycling. Ongoing campus modernization and the removal
 of old facilities generate large amounts of construction and demolition (C&D) debris, including debris from the
 demolition of legacy contaminated facilities.

 2.4.1 Waste Management and Minimization
 Due to the world-class research it conducts, BNL generates a wide range of wastes. Laboratory activities
 generate waste that is characterized as either hazardous, radioactive, mixed, or industrial as per applicable
 Federal and State requirements notably the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) and related New
 York State hazardous waste regulations. These materials come from routine Laboratory research and operational
 activities as well as non-routine construction and demolition activities. Table 2-2 shows waste generation rates
 and trends. Routine and nonroutine wastes are reported separately because the volume of waste from nonroutine
 operations typically overwhelms the routine data.

 Hazardous waste generation from routine operations, which consists of a wide variety of chemical waste from
 research activities, remained within expectations in 2025. Nonroutine hazardous waste during 2025 consisted
 of the usual variety of unused, expired, or contaminated materials generated from maintenance activities and
 building cleanouts. Industrial waste, which is a non-RCRA hazardous waste requiring special handling under
 other regulations, is not broken down as routine and nonroutine because the nature of the generating processes
 can make it difficult to differentiate. In 2025, the industrial waste stream included waste generated from the
 demolition of Building 197. That waste stream had trace mercury contamination that did not meet the toxicity
 characteristic defined in RCRA regulations.

 There were five tons of mixed waste generated from facility maintenance during 2025 and one ton of nonroutine
 mixed waste generated from miscellaneous clean-up improvement activities. Routine radioactive waste

2-8 2025 Site Environmental Report

generated from BNL’s medical isotope research program continued to trend downward. Activities at the facility
have been curtailed for several years for programmatic reasons, so minimal waste is expected next year. A
minimal quantity of nonroutine radioactive waste from the demolition of Building 197 was disposed of in 2025.

BNL also generates municipal solid waste (MSW) and C&D debris that does not have hazardous or radioactive
waste characteristics and is disposed of out of state. Most of this material is diverted from the MSW and C&D
waste streams into BNL’s Pollution Prevention (P2) Program.

The P2 Program reflects national and DOE pollution Table 2-2. Regulated Waste Generation for 2025
prevention policies and represents an ongoing effort
to make P2 and waste minimization an integral part of
BNL’s operating philosophy. Key elements of the P2 Waste Type - Regulated Tons 5 Year Trend
Program include:
 Hazardous Waste Routine (tons) 3

BNL’s EMS establishes objectives and targets to drive pollution prevention and waste reduction as part of overall
sustainable business practices, including the annual P2 Program that provides funds to purchase equipment or
other materials to either reduce an environmental risk or reduce or eliminate a waste stream. Figure 2-1 shows a
breakdown of the various materials that BNL recycles and reuses, with the predominant material being metal.

During 2025, the program funded 13 new proposals, investing approximately $18,600. The proposals involved
reducing spill risk, reducing plastic waste in laboratories, promoting the use of bio-friendly alternative products, and
promoting overall environmentally sustainable business practices. The implementation of P2 opportunities, recycling
programs, and conservation initiatives has reduced both waste volumes and management costs. In 2025, these
efforts resulted in nearly $1.5 million in cost avoidance or savings and approximately 1,503 tons (over 3 million
pounds) of materials being reduced, recycled, reused or sent for energy recovery.

In 2025, BNL collected approximately 1,291 tons of scrap metal for recycling which is consistent with 2024 and
indicative of the C-AD ongoing Removal and Repurposing Project. Cardboard, office paper, bottles and cans,
construction debris, motor oil, lead, automotive and uninterrupted power supply (UPS) batteries, electronic scrap,
and tires were also recycled.

The P2 program has achieved long-term reductions in waste generated by routine operations. However,
population growth and site improvement activities challenge this trend. Overall waste trends can be seen in
Table 2-3 with increases in C&D debris generated due to ongoing site development, including building demolition
and removal of accelerator components and associated equipment from Collider-Accelerator Department’s
(C-AD’s) RHIC facility as part of preparing the facility for the EIC upgrade project. These activities will continue for
the foreseeable future. MSW generation shows a slight increase with expectations that volumes will continue to
increase in the future without the identification and implementation of new diversion opportunities.

 Another contributing factor was the aging process infrastructure. For example, in Building 902 a cooling tower
 gearbox failure in the summer of FY25 prevented proper heat rejection during a critical test. As a temporary
 measure, the cooling tower was operated in a single-pass cooling configuration until the system could be taken
 offline for repairs, resulting in additional potable water consumption.

 In FY26, the RHIC will be taken out of service for upgrades to the EIC. This transition is expected to produce
 an immediate decrease in potable water usage for process loads over the next four to five years. However, it

 2-10 2025 Site Environmental Report

 should be noted that potable water consumption is projected to increase significantly in the following decade
 as additional heat rejection capacity is required to support EIC operations.

2.4.3 Energy Conservation
BNL’s overall energy performance remained relatively stable
between FY24 and FY25. Total site energy intensity increased
slightly, driven primarily by changes in fuel use at the Central Steam
Facility (CSF). While electricity consumption remained generally
consistent, natural gas use decreased modestly as the facility
relied more heavily on fuel oil during periods of cold weather and in
response to operational and economic considerations.

Because the CSF is the site’s primary source of steam and
heating, changes in its fuel mix significantly influence overall energy
consumption patterns. Increased use of fuel oils resulted in a
modest increase in overall fossil fuel energy intensity compared with
the previous year. In addition, aging steam distribution infrastructure
contributed to higher make-up water requirements, reducing
system efficiency and increasing fuel consumption. BNL employee recycling gloves.

 For FY26, energy use is expected to decline due to the final operational run of the RHIC, which will operate
 for a shorter period than in FY25. Key efforts include plans to continue transitioning away from No. 6 fuel
 oil as existing reserves are depleted and regulatory requirements drive the use of cleaner alternative fuels.
 These operational and fuel-transition changes are expected to result in noticeable changes in site-wide
 energy consumption and fuel-use patterns.

 The SRO’s Media and Communications Office manages programs to increase internal stakeholder awareness,
 understanding, and support of Laboratory initiatives; fosters two-way communications; and updates internal
 stakeholders on BNL priorities, news, programs, and events.

 The SRO’s Office of Workforce Development and Science Education (WDSE) manages various education
 initiatives and programs that support the scientific mission at BNL and the DOE. Programs include Summer
 Science Explorations for grades four through twelve, the Science Learning Center, and the DOE’s Workforce
 Development for Teachers and Scientists which sponsors various internship opportunities.

 2.5.1.1 Communications Forums

 To create opportunities for effective dialogue between the Laboratory and its stakeholders, several forums
 for communication and involvement have been established, such as the following:

 § The Community Advisory Council (CAC), established by BNL in 1998, advises
 Laboratory management primarily on environmental, health, and safety issues related
 to BNL that are of importance to the community. The CAC is comprised of 26 member
 organizations and individuals representing civic, education, employee, community,
 environmental, business, and health interests. The CAC sets its own agenda in
 cooperation with the Laboratory, meets six times a year, and is one of the primary ways
 the Laboratory keeps the community informed. Meetings are open to the public and are
 announced on the BNL homepage calendar and on the Stakeholder Relations website
 which links to the CAC webpage, meeting agendas, and past meeting presentations
 and minutes. An opportunity for public comment is provided at each meeting.
 Organizations interested in participating on the CAC are encouraged to attend meetings
 and make their interest known.

2-12 2025 Site Environmental Report

 § Monthly teleconference calls are held with parties to the Laboratory’s Interagency
 Agreement (IAG) and other federal, state, and local regulators to update them on project
 status. The calls also provide the opportunity to gather input and feedback and to discuss
 emerging environmental findings and initiatives.
 § Stakeholder Relations also manages several outreach programs that provide
 opportunities for stakeholders to become familiar with the Laboratory’s facilities and
 research projects. Outreach programs include:

 —Tour Program: Opportunities to learn about BNL are offered to college and university
 groups. Tour groups visit the scientific machines, research facilities, and meet with
 scientists to discuss research. Agendas are developed to meet the interests of the
 groups and often include environmental stewardship issues such as water quality and
 groundwater protection.
 —Explore Brookhaven Open Houses: Held on three Sundays each summer, these open
 houses enable the public to visit BNL science facilities, experience hands-on activities,
 and learn about the Laboratory’s scientific research. In 2025, more than 5,000 visitors
 participated in the program.

The Laboratory also participates in and hosts various outreach events throughout the year such as Science in
the Community programs, a science cafe called PubSci, the Maker’s Faire, and partnering with Stony Brook
University on their “Comms/University Day.” Internally, All Hands staff meetings are held throughout the year to
keep employees apprised of Laboratory updates and new programs.

The SRO’s Media and Communications Office issues press releases to news and media outlets and the Internal
Communications Office publishes electronic and printed weekly employee newsletters, such as Brookhaven This
Week and The Brookhaven Digest. In addition, a Director’s Office web-based publication, the Monday Memo, is
issued bi-weekly to employees and focuses on topics important to the Laboratory population. The Laboratory
maintains an informative website at where these publications, as well as extensive information about BNL’s science
and operations, past and present, are posted. In addition, employees and the community can subscribe to the
Laboratory’s e-mail news service.

 2.5.1.2 Community Involvement in Cleanup Projects

 In 2025, BNL updated stakeholders on the progress of environmental clean-up projects, additional initiatives,
 and health and safety issues via briefings, and presentations given at CAC meetings. These topics included
 the following:
 § Environmental Updates: In 2025, the CAC received additional environmental updates
 such as the general status of the groundwater contaminant plumes and remediation
 systems, emerging contaminants PFAS and 1,4 Dioxane in groundwater, and updates on
 the mercury remediation project and the Site Environmental Report.
 § Discovery Technology Overview: Overview of the new directorate was given by the
 new Associate Lab Director, Gabriella Carini. The new directorate focuses on four areas:
 1) Instrumentation, 2) Microelectronics, 3) Quantum Information Science, Technology and
 Engineering and 4) Accelerator Science and Technology.
 § New Long Island Rail Road (LIRR) Yaphank-BNL Station Opening: An overview was
 provided by representatives of Metropolitan Transportation Authority (MTA)/LIRR about
 construction of a new Yaphank station. The station was moved closer to the Laboratory
 and provides rail access from New York City and New Jersey’s airports.
 § Energy Storage (batteries) Overview: Amy Marschilok, Manager of the Energy Storage
 Division and Energy Systems Division, as well as a scientist in BNL’s Interdisciplinary
 Science Department (ISB), provided an update on electrochemical energy storage. She
 noted that the ISB’s research is centered on three main areas: energy efficiency, grid
 modernization, and energy storage.
 § Update on the Center for Functional Nanomaterials (CFN): Chuck Black, Director
 of the CFN, gave an update about the exciting science and research taking place to
 accelerate nanomaterial discoveries of the 21st century.
 § EIC Tunnel Cooling Water Design Update: Charlie Folz, Infrastructure Division Director
 for the EIC, provided an update on the EIC tunnel cooling water design. He explained
 that systems for magnet and vacuum beam pipe cooling water are needed to cool
 components for the Electron Storage Ring.
 § Natural and Cultural Resource Updates: The CAC received updates on BNL’s natural
 resources, such as deer and fire management, as well as a general overview of the
 Cultural Resource Program, including historical buildings, Camp Upton trenches, and
 remnants from historical homesteads.

 3.2
 National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) Assessments
 NEPA regulations require federal agencies to evaluate the environmental effects of proposed major federal
 activities. The prescribed evaluation process ensures that the proper level of environmental review is performed
 before an irreversible commitment of resources is made. During 2025, environmental evaluations were
 completed for 175 proposed projects at BNL. A total of 171 projects were considered minor actions requiring
 no additional documentation. Four projects were addressed by submitting a notification form to the DOE, which
 determined that the projects were covered by existing “Categorical Exclusions” (per 10 CFR 1021).

3-10 2025 Site Environmental Report

3.3
Safe Drinking Water Act (SDWA)
The extraction and distribution of drinking water are regulated under the federal SDWA. In New York State,
implementation of the SDWA is delegated to the New York State Department of Health (NYSDOH) and
administered locally by Suffolk County Department of Health Services (SCDHS). Because BNL provides potable
water to more than 25 full-time residents, it is subject to the same requirements as a municipal water supplier.
Monitoring requirements are prescribed annually by SCDHS, and a Potable Water Sampling and Analysis Plan
(Bruno, 2025) is prepared by the Laboratory to comply with these requirements.

3.3.1 Potable Water
The Laboratory has six water supply wells for on-site distribution of potable water, four of which were active
during 2025. As required by NYSDOH regulations, BNL monitors the potable wells regularly for bacteria,
inorganics, organics, and pesticides. The Laboratory also voluntarily monitors drinking water supplies for
radiological contaminants yearly. BNL’s drinking water and the supply and distribution system were in full
compliance with all applicable county, state, and federal regulations regarding drinking water quality, monitoring,
operations, and reporting in 2025.

BNL has determined that all six supply wells are highly susceptible to PFAS associated with the Laboratory’s
past use of firefighting foam. In August 2020, New York State established drinking water standards of 10 ng/L
(parts per trillion) for PFAS compounds perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) and perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS).
PFOS and several other PFAS compounds have been detected in supply wells at concentrations above 10
ng/L. To address this contamination, BNL has placed back into service granular activated carbon (GAC) filtration
systems at three wells to remove PFAS before the water is released into the distribution system. BNL also ended
the operation of two supply wells (Wells 4 and 6) that do not have GAC filters.

All in service potable supply wells and water leaving the Water Treatment Plant are now tested on a quarterly
basis for PFAS in accordance with applicable county, state, and federal regulations and are in full compliance.

To ensure that consumers are informed about the quality of
Laboratory-supplied potable water, BNL publishes a Consumer
Confidence Report (CCR) in May of each year, a deadline
stipulated by the SDWA. This report provides information
regarding source water supply system, the analytical tests
conducted and the detected contaminants which are then
compared to federal drinking water standards. The CCR also
describes the measures the Laboratory takes to protect its water
source and limit consumer exposure to contaminants.

The CCR is distributed to all BNL employees and on-site
residents, either in paper form or electronically at
http://www.bnl.gov/water/.

3.3.2 Cross-Connection Control
The SDWA requires that public water suppliers implement
practices to protect the water supply from sanitary hazards.
One of the safety requirements is to rigorously prevent cross-
connections between the potable water supply and facility piping
systems. Cross-connection control is the installation of control

 2025 Site Environmental Report 3-11

 devices (e.g., double-check valves, reduced pressure zone valves, etc.) at the interface between a facility and the
 domestic water main. Cross-connection control devices are required at all facilities where hazardous materials are
 used in a manner that could result in their accidental introduction into the domestic water system, especially under
 low-pressure conditions. In addition, secondary cross-connection controls at the point of use are recommended
 to protect users within a specific facility from hazards that may be posed by intrafacility operations.

 During 2025, the Laboratory inspected 292 cross-connection control devices, including primary devices installed
 at interfaces to the potable water main and secondary control devices at the point of use. If a problem with a
 cross-connection device is encountered during testing, the device is repaired and re-tested to ensure proper
 function. Copies of the cross-connection device test reports are filed with SCDHS each year.

 3.3.3 Underground Injection Control
 Underground Injection Control (UIC) wells are regulated under the SDWA. At the Laboratory, UICs include
 drywells, cesspools, septic tanks, and leaching pools, all of which are classified by EPA as Class V injection
 wells. Proper management of UIC devices is vital for protecting underground sources of drinking water. In New
 York State, the UIC program is implemented through EPA because NYSDEC has not adopted UIC regulatory
 requirements (Note: New York State regulates the discharges of pollutants to cesspools under the SPDES
 program). Under EPA’s UIC program, all Class V injection wells are authorized by rule and must be included in an
 inventory maintained with the agency.

 In addition to the UICs maintained for routine Laboratory discharges of sanitary waste and stormwater,
 UICs also are maintained at several on- and off-site treatment facilities used for groundwater remediation.
 Contaminated groundwater is treated and then returned to the aquifer via drywells, injection wells, or recharge
 basins. Discharges to these UICs are also authorized by rule rather than by permit. Under the authorized by
 rule requirements, a separate inventory is maintained for these treatment facilities and is periodically updated
 whenever a new device is added or closed.

 No UIC installations occurred in 2025. BNL’s total UIC inventory at the end of 2025 was 133.

 3.4
 Preventing and Reporting Spills
 Federal, state, and local regulations are in place to address the management of storage facilities containing
 chemicals, petroleum, and other hazardous materials. The regulations include specifications for the design of
 storage facilities, requirements for written plans relating to unplanned releases, and requirements for reporting
 releases that do occur. BNL’s compliance with these regulations is further described in the following sections.

 3.4.1 Preventing Oil Pollution and Spills
 As required by the Oil Pollution Act, BNL maintains a Spill Prevention Control and Countermeasures (SPCC)
 Plan as a condition of its license to store petroleum fuel (Bruno, 2024). The purpose of this plan is to provide
 information regarding release prevention measures, the design of storage facilities, and maps detailing storage
 facility locations. The plan also outlines mitigating and remedial actions that would be taken in the event of a
 major spill. BNL’s SPCC plan is filed with NYSDEC, EPA, and DOE, and must be updated every five years. BNL
 remained in full compliance with SPCC requirements in 2025.

3-12 2025 Site Environmental Report

3.4.2 Emergency Reporting Requirements
The Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act (EPCRA) and Title III of the Superfund Amendments
and Reauthorization Act require that facilities report inventories and releases of certain chemicals that exceed specific
release thresholds. Community Right-to-Know requirements are codified under 40 CFR Parts 355, 370, and 372.
Table 3-3 summarizes the applicability of the regulations to BNL.
The Laboratory complied with these requirements through the submittal of Tier II and Tier III Reports required under
EPCRA Sections 302, 303, 311, 312, and 313. In fulfillment of the Tier II requirements, BNL submitted an inventory
of 40 on-site chemicals (with thresholds greater than 10,000 pounds or 500 pounds for acutely toxic materials) via
E-Plan, the New York State-approved computer-based submittal program.
To satisfy the requirements of the Tier III submittal, the Laboratory submitted its data via the EPA-approved web based
Toxic Release Inventory (TRI-ME) submittal program. During 2025, BNL reported releases of lead (about 28,000
pounds), mercury (about 28 pounds), polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) (about five pounds), benzo(g,h,i)perylene
(less than one pound), polycyclic aromatic compounds (less than one pound), asbestos (friable) (6,960 pounds), and
2,2-Dichloro-1,1,1-trifluoroethane (HCFC-123) (five pounds). Releases of lead, PCBs, asbestos, and mercury were
in the form of shipments of waste for off-site recycling or disposal. Releases of benzo(g,h,i)perylene and polycyclic
aromatic compounds were byproducts of the combustion of fuel oils and were well below any regulatory limits.
Fugitive losses of the refrigerant HCFC-123 were from centrifugal chillers on-site. In 2025, there were no releases of
extremely hazardous substances reportable under Part 304.

3.4.3 Spills and Releases
When a spill of hazardous material occurs, Laboratory and contractor personnel are required to immediately
notify the BNL Fire Rescue Group, whose members are trained to respond to such releases. Fire Rescue’s
initial response is to contain and control any release and to notify additional response personnel (e.g., BNL
environmental professionals, industrial hygienists, etc.). Environmental professionals reporting to the scene
assess the spill for environmental impact and determine if it is reportable to regulatory agencies. Any release of
petroleum products to soil must be reported to both NYSDEC and SCDHS, and any release affecting surface
water is also reported to the EPA National Response Center. In addition, a release of more than five gallons of
petroleum product to impermeable surfaces or containment areas must be reported to NYSDEC and SCDHS.
Spills of chemicals in quantities greater than the CERCLA-reportable limits must be reported to the EPA National
Response Center, NYSDEC, and SCDHS. Remediation of spills is conducted, as necessary, to prevent impacts
to the environment, minimize human health exposures, and restore the site.
